## Dun pudding

A dun pudding was a rare deadly pudding that was adapted to arid deserts.

Description
Dun puddings had a yellow color, similar to that of sand. This made it easy for them to conceal themselves in desert environments.

Behavior
Dun puddings were scavengers, but preferred to consume animal and vegetable matter. If such a meal was not available, the puddings could survive off of the silicates found in sand.

Combat
When in combat, these puddings would use their internal secretions to dissolve their foes' equipment. They were very fast at dissolving all leather items, but were slower at eating through metals.

Ecology
Though usually found alone, dun puddings could form groups of up to four.
Habitats
In the Abyss, dun puddings could be found in Shedaklah, the Slime Pits, 222nd layer of the Abyss.
Relationships
Dun puddings were a favored monster of Zuggtmoy.

## Dungeons & Dragons: Dark Alliance

Dungeons & Dragons: Dark Alliance is a video game developed by Tuque Games and distributed by Wizards of the Coast, released on June 22, 2021. The game is set in the Icewind Dale region.

Story

After Akar Kessell's army is defeated 1356 DR, hordes of powerful creatures invade the northern region of Icewind Dale in an attempt to locate the Crystal Shard.

Gameplay
The game includes both single-player and multiplayer modes. In the single-player mode, the player can choose any of the four characters to control, and swap between them. The multiplayer mode allows for online co-op for up to four players.

## Duhlark's glasstrike

Duhlark's glasstrike was a spell used by sorcerers and wizards.

Effects
When cast, this spell turned a targeted creature or object into glass. When transforming objects, it turned up to  of it's volume into glass. The spell had no effect on enchanted or magical items. When the spell expired, the transformed creature or object returned to their normal state. If they suffered damaged while transformed, that damage was evident after the spell expired.

The reverse of this spell, called Duhlark's glassremove, negated the effects of a previously cast glasstrike spell.

Components
In addition to verbal and somatic components, the spell required a focus, a piece of a mirror.

## Duruch'i-lin

Duruch'i-lin were massive creatures native to Kara-Tur. The female was called a ch'i-lin and the male was called a duru.

Description
Duruch'i-lin looked like gigantic, colorful horses. Female duruch'i-lin were much larger than the males, with ch'i-lin able to reach a height of . Both ch'i-lin and duru had the same appearance, with the difference being the color of their undersides. A ch'i-lin's belly was bright yellow, while a duru's belly was blue. A duruch'i-lin resembled a unicorn, with a  blunt horn that protruded from its forehead. They had teeth and a jaw structure that was similar to a wolf. A duruch'i-lin's coat was colorful, covered in spots of black, blue, red, white, and yellow.

Combat
Duruch'i-lin were gentle and peaceful creatures. They avoided violence at all costs and only resorted to it as a last resort. In addition to magic, a duruch'i-lin used its hooves, horn, and sharp teeth as weapons.

Abilities
The duruch'i-lin's horn was able to launch magic bolts several times a day. They had the innate ability to duplicate the effects of invisibility and plane shift spells at will and crystalbrittle and bless several times a day. Duruch'i-lin were immune to every type of poison, as well as any charm, death, or hold-based spells. An aura permanently surrounded a duruch'i-lin, similar to the deflection spell. This aura was gold for ch'i-lin and silver for duru.

Society
Duruch'i-lin had a kinship with all living creatures, especially those of the forest. They avoided civilization and were rarely seen by humans. Despite this, humans in Kara-Tur associated duruch'i-lin with longevity, good health, and fortune.

Habitat
Duruch'i-lin were solitary and did not live with others of their kind, nor did they have permanent lairs. When one wanted privacy, it shifted to another plane, sometimes for up to a decade. They claimed and protected large areas of a forest, up to .

Ecology
Duruch'i-lin rarely walked upon the ground itself, in fear of damaging vegetation or harming small creatures and insects. Instead, they constantly glided through the air. They consumed only minerals and drank only pure stream water. Duruch'i-lin were extremely long-lived creatures.

The mating ritual of duruch'i-lin was lengthy and complex. When a duru wished to mate with a ch'i-lin, he brought her to his territory, and she was greeted by a gathering of forest animals. Each creature in his territory was represented. The reason for this gathering was for the representatives to approve of the ch'i-lin. If they liked her and approval was granted, the duru then introduced the ch'i-lin directly to the Celestial Bureaucracy for final approval. If approval was given, then they both then shifted to another plane, where they spent the next five years sharing every detail of their long lives. They gave birth to a single child and remained on the plane until the child reached maturity, which took about fifty years. Once they all returned to the Prime Material Plane, the family split up, each going its own way.

History
Legend stated that the first ch'i-lin was a magical blending of two now extinct creatures, the ch'i and the lin. The former was a silver-antlered stag with emerald eyes and the latter a golden warhorse with earthquake-causing steps. This first ch'i-lin rose from the waters of the Akano River in Wa and presented itself to the nation's first Emperor, Kochi, in the first year of his rule. Upon the ch'i-lin's back were a series of characters, from which Kochi created the first written form of the Wa-an language.

Uses
An individual who touched the horn of a living duruch'i-lin temporarily gained the ability to cast the omen spell. This affect lasted only for an hour. The coat of a dead duruch'i-lin was a highly sought after item, as it retained its deflection ability. The horn could be ground into a powder and was used as an antidote that was effective against all poisons.

## Ducal Castle

The Ducal Castle, also known as Daggerford Castle, was the ancestral home of the Daggerford family, as well as the seat of power for the dukes and duchess that served as Daggerford's heads–of–state.

Location
The castle was built atop a small hill in the eastern half of town. It was surrounded by the open area referred to as the Commons.

Structure
The stone keep was three stories tall and surrounded by a stone wall that was about two-thirds its height.

Between the keep and the castle walls was a small courtyard that held the duke's smithy, the stables, several gardens and their private grounds. The gate leading out to the rest of town remained open to its citizens at all times.

Set within the hill beneath the castle were dungeons that dated back to the Barony of Blood, and its ruler Artor Morlin.

History
Construction of the castle began in the, by the local folk hero Tyndal "Daggerford". It was originally built as a simple wooden fortress, but was eventually burnt to the ground by orcs. Duke Tyndal rebuilt his castle out of stone and hunted down the orcs responsible for its previous destruction. The residents of the castle began to build outside of its walls, and eventually the town of Daggerford was formed.

When the dwarves of Clan Ironeater moved to Daggerford in the 13 century DR, they offered their masonry skills to the town and its duke. They removed the last portions of the castle that were still made of wood, such as the guard towers atop its walls, and rebuilt them in stone.

## Dunbridges

Dunbridges was a village in High Emmerock. It was located in the north-eastern Border Kingdoms.

Geography
Dunbridges was located in the middle of small hills covered in verdant pastures. The village was build around several mills and this resulted in the village being saturated with millponds and springs. The village was known for its many bridges.

Government
The village was ruled by an eleven-member council and a speaker. The council was called the Mouth and comprised local merchants nominated by the residents of the village. Each councillor was elected to a term of three years and could only serve two consecutive terms at a time. The speaker of the council was given the title Lord of the Bridges.

Trade
Dunbridges offered merchants several trade goods for export. Dunbradar cheese was made from goat's milk and nuts. The millponds and streams within the village were well stocked with fish. Dunbridges was know for it's high-quality woollen goods, especially the unique blankets weaved in the village. These blankets provided excellent camouflage due to its weave pattern and color. One of the local adventuring groups, the Merciless Manticoreslayers, distills a whisky called Slayers's Fire. It has proven popular with merchants and can be found in taverns across Faerûn.

Defenses
The village had a modest constabulary of a dozen men. The constabulary, called the Fingers, was led by the Lord High Protector. Defense is also provided by three local adventuring companies. The primary defensive concerns for the village were dealing with the occasional brigands and repel invasions from Thuntar, who attempt to annex the village about once every ten years.

Inhabitants
In the mid–14 century DR, the important inhabitants of the village were:
 Anarassa Ghoeble, Lord of the Bridges.
 Tharorgaun Tarntree, Lord High Protector.
 Khaladaen, leader of the Merciless Manticoreslayers.
 Belabranta Sheltress, leader of the Company of the Bent Blade.
 Haeronth Blaedaerer, spokesperson of the Deadhelms of Dunbridges.

## Dung snake

Dung snakes were a species of aquatic constrictor snake. They were among the species considered serpents.

Description
Dung snakes had brown scales that were coated in a slime that gave off a strong odour.

Biology
When faced with cold weather conditions, the body of a dung snake would exude a protective slime around itself. This slime provided dung snakes with a measure of resistance to cold. They also had a natural immunity to disease and were capable of quickly healing from an injury.

Combat
Dung snakes attacked their prey while in water. They wrapped themselves around its prey, constricting and dragging them underwater. They anchored themselves to its prey by biting them with their sharp fangs. Once submerged, dung snakes held on to its prey until they drowned.

Ecology
Dung snakes got their name from their instinct to seek out warmth in compost piles and dung heaps when the weather turned cold. They would burrow deep into these compost piles, dung heaps, cesspools, or other source of decomposition-based warmth and sleep there until either disturbed or warm weather returned to the local area.
Habitat
Dung snakes were found in cesspools, marshes and wetlands. They were known to live within the Marsh of Chelimber, the Rat Hills and the River Sargauth in Undermountain.
Uses
Dung snakes were a common familiar used by spellcasters.

## Dusty

Dusty was a reversed cantrip that neophyte wizards could learn. It caused a thin layer of grimy dust to cover an area.

Effects
This cantrip had a range of  and could affect a circular area  across. When cast, the area was covered in a film of dust and grime as if it had been left unoccupied and undisturbed for a long time.

Components
This cantrip required verbal and somatic components to cast. The caster made a quiet humming sound while moving both hands as if shaking or sprinkling dust over something.

## Dusk elf

Dusk elves were a subset of elves that took no side in the War of the Seldarine. Gaining the enmity of Corellon, these elves were accepted and sheltered from the Protector's vengeance by Sehanine Moonbow.

Notable Dusk Elves
 The Velikovnas were a family of dusk elves that inhabited Barovia. Among their number were Patrina Velikovna, one of Strahd von Zarovich's former brides who haunted the catacombs of Castle Ravenloft as a banshee, and her brother, Kasimir Velikov.
 Rahadin was the chamberlain of Castle Ravenloft. He aided King Barov von Zarovich in subjugating his fellow dusk elves, and served as a general under Strahd after Barov's death until they settled in the valley of Barovia.

## Dust

Dust was a useful cantrip that acolyte wizards could learn. It removed all fine particles and grit from a small area, sending it elsewhere.

Effects
The target of this spell had to be within  of the caster and only worked on open surfaces within  of the target point. All tiny dust and grit particles on shelves, walls, furniture, floors, etc. within the area of effect were transported to an unknown location. This cantrip could not remove dust-like substances from inside closed containers like a snuff box or a makeup jar.

Components
The cantrip required verbal and somatic components to cast. A hand was waved back and forth while performing a slow inhalation of breath for a few seconds.

## Dust shield

Dust shield was a southern magic divine spell available to worshipers of Geb.

Effects
When cast, the spell created an impenetrable barrier of dust. Once the barrier was in place, it could not be moved by the caster. Only the dispel magic spell or a mental command from the caster could remove the barrier before the spell expires. The caster was able to cast the spell around one of their arms, providing a full body shield. If a caster cast it in this fashion, they were unable to wield a shield or in conjunction with the shield spell.

Components
In addition to a verbal component, this spell required material components, a small amount of dust along with the caster's holy symbol.

## Durlin

Durlin was the last brewmaster of Myth Drannor who perished during the Weeping War and whose ghostly apparition could be seen and heard haunting the places he loved in life.

Possessions
Durlin left behind a few items that belonged to him, he hid his pair of gloves of courage in a barrel in the room he used to play chess at, and his favorite weapon, the brewmaster's cudgel was used by one of the lizardfolk that found in the Dungeons' ruins.

History
As the brewmaster, Durlin was maintaining the Shrine of Hanseath in the Halls of Light and was in charge of the city's sacred brewery. His tireless work could still be witnessed all across many levels of the Dwarven Dungeons centuries after his death. Many casks of his holy brews still possessed their magical properties even as late as 1369 DR. In life, Durlin spent countless hours playing Chess with his best friend Drake in the Halls of Light's Chess Room.

In 1369 DR his ghost helped the heroes from Phlan who arrived at Myth Drannor to oppose the Cult of the Dragon's evil plan. Durlin guided them to his hidden magic items in the Halls of Light.

## Durren's workshop

Durren's Workshop was a small and simple building that belonged to a famous sculptor and philanthropist Durren Strongshatter who lived in the pre-Weeping War Myth Drannor.

Location
The workshop itself stood in the Nightingale Court district of Myth Drannor, nearby to the Castle Cormanthor and adjacent to the Street of the Evening Star.

Structure
Durren's workshop was a very humble squat building that served as a home and his workshop. The front of the building was paved and the walls were constructed with mortared rock, and the path leading to it was littered with a couple of dozens of his mundane and more exotic projects, with some being piled on the roof of the building.

Interior
The interior of the workshop was very simple, it held no treasure of the olden days, as Durren did not keep any reward for his work, instead, spending it on aiding the up and coming and struggling dwarven communities across Faerun. The most valuable thing in the whole building were his masterwork sculpting tools.

History
In the final stretch of the Weeping War, Durren Strongshatter, already quite old, stood against the invading forces with his pickax, defending his home and his work, and was slain by a yugoloth. Upon his death, the mythal's arcane energies, mixed with the pouring magic of the war, and Durren's own creative spark, his creations came to life.

The newly living gargoyles avenger their creator by dragging the yugoloth responsible to the workshop's rooftop where it was twisted, turned, and deformed into a horrific version of itself and turned into stone.

These non-evil gargoyles continued living in the ruined city. They were known to return to live every few years just to grab a hold of a fiend somewhere in the city and do to it the same thing they did to the killer of their creator. On occasion, adventurers that came across Durren's workshop were able to bride some of his gargoyled with gems to have a conversation.

Inhabitants
Durren Strongshatter was a multi-talented sculptor but was especially passionate about carving gargoyles. His gargoyles were created in a multitude of shapes, humanoid, cat-like, dog-like, completely monstrous, and uniquely abstract. His gargoyles remained in the ruins of the city and the workshop itself for the centuries that followed their creator's death.

## Durren Strongshatter

Durren Strongshatter was a famous dwarven sculptor and a philanthropist who died in the final days of the Weeping War of Myth Drannor leaving behind a legacy of uniquely-shaped peaceful gargoyles.

Personality
Even though Durren Strongshatter was renowned for his skills, he lived a humble frugal life, using the money he made with his art to sponsor struggling dwarven communities throughout Faerûn.

Abilities
Durren possessed some combat skills that he developed much earlier in his long life, he kept his military pick as a proof.

The most noteworthy skills the old Strongshatter had was the amazing ability to recreate anyone's likeness, chiseled out of stone of any kind, carved out of wood, or ice, or shaped out of metal. The dignitaries and nobles of Myth Drannor paid him handsomely for his work. His true passion was in creating gargoyles of various shapes, from humanoid and animalistic, to monstrous and abstractly shaped. His humble workshop was littered with many such carved creatures.

History
Durren Strongshatter picked up his military pick, untouched for over hundred years, in defense of his humble workshop from the advancing Army of Darkness. In the final moments of the Weeping War, when Myth Drannor's defenses were at last overwhelmed, Durren was bested and killed in battle by a yugoloth. His death, creative spark, wild magics of the war, and the energies of the city's mythal somehow breathed live into Durren's beloved creations. The gargoyles seized the killer of their creator, dragged him to the workshop's roof. There, the yugoloth was twisted and broken into a horrifically deformed version of itself, and turned to stone.

In centuries following the destruction of Myth Drannor, Durren's gargoyles were known to return to live every few years, and fly to hunt for fiends, granting them the same fate as the yugoloth who killed Strongshatter. These gargoyles could be bribed with gemstones by adventurers exploring the ruins to share information or other aid.

## Dungeon Master Option: High-Level Campaigns

Dungeon Master Option: High-Level Campaigns is a 2-edition Advanced Dungeons & Dragons accessory.

Description
Dungeon Master Option: High-Level Campaigns focuses on providing guidelines and rules for running campaigns that involve high-level characters, which usually means characters above the 10 level. It introduces new rules and options to enhance the gameplay experience for high-level characters. This includes more powerful spells, abilities, and equipment suitable for these experienced adventurers.

The book provides advice and ideas for creating adventures and challenges that are appropriately scaled for high-level characters. This is crucial because such characters can easily overcome obstacles and threats that would be formidable for lower-level characters. It includes guidance on further character development, such as epic-level progression, expanding on the characters' abilities and influence in the game.

The book also offers tips on world-building and managing a campaign that includes high-level characters, ensuring that the game remains balanced and engaging. It has advice on storytelling and narrative development, which is key in maintaining interest in a campaign where characters already possess great power and resources.

Contents
 Chapter 1: The Seven Maxims
 Chapter 2: Adventures
 Chapter 3: Spells and Magical Items
 Chapter 4: Creating Magical Items
 Chapter 5: Magical Duels
 Chapter 6: True Dweomers
 Chapter 7: High-Level Characters
 Appendix: Spell Dueling Characteristics

The book includes 52 tables.

## Dulkaster and Sons

Dulkaster and Sons was a wealthy and successful family trading organizations operating on the Sword Coast in the late 14
 century DR.

Activities
By the 1370s DR, Dulkaster and Sons has been operating a profitable business managing trade across the Sword Coast for centuries.

Base of Operations
Dulkaster and Sons' main offices were located in Waterdeep and its family members often traveled for business purposes, sometimes staying away from the main office for months at a time.

History
In 1372 DR, Egbert Dulkaster the Third was visiting Daggerford. While he was conducting business there he stayed at the Chateau Elite.

Members
Egbert Dulkaster the Third, the pompous rich man who appreciated wealth, and fine fashionable silk clothes circa 1372 DR.

## Dungrunglung

Dungrunglung was a settlement of grungs located in the jungles of Chult in the late 15 century DR. Dungrunglung was surrounded by a tall maze of magical, twisted vines. The interior consisted of a large pond with narrow edges dotted with tents. In the center of the lake there was a giant shrine dedicated to the goddess Nangnang.

## Durdyn Teh'kinrellz

Durdyn Teh'kinrellz was the weapon master of House Teh'Kinrellz of Ched Nasad in 1370 DR.

History
Durdyn took on the female commoner, Relirva Indagley'Belash, and she served under him. He gave her the opportunity to study creatures around Ched Nasad, such as choldrith and chitines. Her efforts led to the death of a rival houses's secondboy, and she was granted greater privileges by her house.

## Dungeonology

Dungeonology is an officially licensed tome based upon the original tabletop roleplaying game, Dungeons & Dragons. Narrated by Volo, the author of dozens of D&D guidebooks, the book features an introduction from the great wizard Elminster, written by his creator, Ed Greenwood himself.

## Dulgan Proudfist

Dulgan Proudfist was a cunning member of the Proudfist tribe of half-orcs who lived on the Sword Coast North during the late 15 century DR.

Personality
Unlike his tribe's chieftain who valued strength, Dulgan was a more subtle individual. He respected those who demonstrated shrewdness and cunning above others.

History
In the, Dulgan and the Proudfists served Vansi Bloodscar in the Many-Arrows occupation of the River District of Neverwinter and fought against the Neverwinter Guard. When a half-elf adventurer sought to prove their worth to the Proudfists in an effort to persuade them to change sides, Dulgan challenged them to find his hidden trinkets.

Activities
As a way to amuse himself, Dulgan liked to hide small caches of trinkets throughout the River District, unbeknownst to the orcs.

## Dun-Tharos

Dun-Tharos was a city located near the center of the Rawlinswood, which was the seat of various men and women known as Nentyarchs.

History
Construction of Dun-Tharos as a traditional citadel of black stone was completed in  over the ruins of Narathmault by Thargaun Crell, the first Nentyarch of Tharos (later Narfell). He named his realm, and his citadel, in honor of his father, who was the one to rediscover Narathmault forty-five years before. Dun-Tharos saw seventeen generations of Narfelli Nentyarchs occupy it before the end of the Great Conflagration in the  that destroyed the Empire of Narfell.

It was not until the, when the Great Druid of Leth erected a tree-fortress around the ruins of the old citadel. The Great Druid took up the title of Nentyarch and passed the title on to their successors.

The Circle of Leth controlled Dun-Tharos for almost six hundred and fifty years until, on Midwinter of the, the Chosen of Talona, known as the Rotting Man led an army of corrupted druids and volodnis to drive out the Circle.

The Rotting Man killed and animated the fortress's trees and made Dun-Tharos his home. This situation lasted only sixteen months however, until the Rotting Man too was driven out. Unfortunately, the conflict that led to this occurrence, also roused the demonic energies that dwelt beneath the citadel.

Still dead and rotting from the influence of Talona, the place infested with demons, Dun-Tharos became a place of immense danger, ruled by several petty demon lords and their fiend-worshiping mortal cultists, the Fraternity of Tharos.

Structure and Sample Rooms
Dun-Tharos had once boasted great plazas, and temples dedicated to abyssal powers. There had been statuary, facades, and such, but they had crumbled by 1373 DR. Though the encroaching wilderness had reduced most of the city to stagnant pools and rubble, the outline of what had once been a city was still vaguely visible.

Tunnels beneath the city stretched for miles in all directions. In them, wooden doors with pull-rings were common; as of, they had held up against the passage of time, proving very resistant to the usual ravages. Stone faces were often carved around door frames. Clutter and bizarre noises were common throughout the ruins. At least one of the underground rooms held obelisks, and another one, iron blades rising from the ground just like obelisks, though they had crumbled from rust and use. Another room in the underground tunnels beneath the city was covered in murals of vile acts, some of which were bleached of all color. It was a trap, where anyone who touched or got too close to the pictography in the ground would be absorbed into it. Another room held a number of salt-like crystals of numerous sizes, which were actually an otherwise unknown kind of demon.

The Barrow of the Queen Abiding connected these tunnels to a location just past the Arches of Xenosi, several miles away from the city, through a secret door. The Sighing Vault was hidden somewhere in these labyrinthine tunnels. The Lurker in the Middle was also lurking amongst those corridors.

The Close, a fortress made of magically grown trees, had been added to aboveground Dun-Tharos in  by the Circle of Leth. The head druid of that circle began to call himself the Nentyarch by that time.

Inhabitants
Queen Abiding
Eschar
Lurker in the Middle

## Dungeon

A dungeon was a kind of environment or structure found on Toril, in Realmspace, and other distant planes. Though often hard to find and extremely dangerous, they were a much sought-after source of treasure for adventurers, tales for bards, and lore for scholars. They also served as the homes of bandits and monsters, repositories of long-lost magic, and the tombs of long-dead gods.

Definition
The original definition of a dungeon in Faerûn was that of a place used as a prison, but as adventurers explored ruins old and new, the term "dungeon" came to broaden immensely and included any resilient old place of stone that involved monsters, treasure, and exploration. As a result, it was commonly understood in Faerûn that dungeons could be anything from the cellars of ruined homes to vast underground labyrinths.

In the Realms, most dungeons were quite ancient and often continuously inhabited over the centuries by various creatures, intelligent or otherwise. This led to them becoming some combination of battlefield, ruin, biosphere, and habitation that separated them from a common ruin or animal den and created a distinct place often far removed from the surrounding natural or urban environment.

Dominion over dungeons and underground spaces was originally held by Ibrandul, the Skulking God, though he was killed by Shar during the Time of Troubles due to her jealous possessiveness over dark places. She proceeded to take over his portfolio and impersonate him, controlled his priesthood, and otherwise ruled over dungeons and the subterranean world using his name.

Ultimately, a dungeon was a confined space filled with danger and treasure in which adventurers practiced their trade, though exceptions to this existed.The reader is advised not to think too hard about the potential interpretations of this loose definition, as it leads to ends such as "Is an armed bank robbery a dungeon?" and "Is life a dungeon?". This common understanding and acceptance led to the High Sun Games of Neverwinter in the 1490s DR, which used an imitation-dungeon arena stocked with real monsters such as mimics, displacer beasts, and gelatinous cubes.As the arena held real perils, was involved in an evil plot, and was traversed by adventurers actively on an adventure, it could be argued that this fake dungeon was actually a real dungeon.

Notable Dungeons

Some dungeons were extremely well known in the Realms, or had extraordinary properties compared to their fellows:

 Dragonspear Castle, a great castle on the edge of the High Moor overtaken by successive invaders.
 Glaur, a fortress in Laerakond built for the Dawn War before the separation of Abeir from Toril.
 The Haunted Halls, an extensive fortified hideout in northern Cormyr.
 The House of Stone, a dwarven citadel and lethal trap.
 Myth Drannor, the legendary ruins of the greatest elven city in Faerûn.
 The Paths of the Doomed, a bizarre magical labyrinth beneath the Thaymount of Thay.
 Shoonach, the vast sprawling urban ruins of the massive capital of the Shoon Imperium.
 Stardock, an inhabited asteroid in the Tears of Selûne taken over by successive groups.
 The Tomb of the Nine Gods, an extensive and malevolent death trap hidden under the ruins of Omu.
 Undermountain, perhaps the most famous and elaborate dungeon (or series of dungeons) in all of Faerûn.

Dungeons could even be found in entirely different worlds, with some of them gaining legendary fame (or infamy):
 Castle Greyhawk and the Ghost Tower of Inverness on Oerth.
 Castle Ravenloft in Barovia.
 Mordain's Hall on Eberron.
 The Bleeding Citadel in Avernus.
 The many death-traps built by Acererak, such as his Tomb of Horrors.

Naming

Many dungeons possessed their original names thanks to being well-known or at least well-researched. Places such as Dragonspear Castle and Shoonach were never lost to time and remained well known, while others were complete mysteries to all but the most well-informed sages. Those that were forgotten then gained new names based on their new inhabitants or some distinguishing feature that explorers noted on the way in and out. The Crumbling Stair was named for the sole above-ground remnant of its structure, while the Halls of the Hammer were named for the prominent animated, flying warhammer that patrolled it.

Some dungeons were explicitly named as dungeons, usually because their original name and history were long lost. Those included the Dungeon of Death, Dungeon of Swords, Dungeons of Torment, Dungeon of the Crypt, Dungeon of the Hark, Dungeon of the Ruins, Dungeon of the Shield, Dwarven Dungeons, and Nameless Dungeon. The Dungeon of the Inquisitor was a proper dungeon used for prisoners, but also contained dangerous, uncontrolled tunnels that were home to monsters.

Dungeons often took on multiple names as different groups discovered and inhabited them. For example, the dwarven hold of Andalbruin was peacefully abandoned, then later Netherese settlers built atop it and named it Selskartur before it too fell to ruin. When it was finally rediscovered, it was known simply as the Dungeon of the Ruins.

Types of Dungeons
Builders

Dwarves
Dwarves built many of the most famous and complex underground structures of Faerûn both on the surface and deep in the Underdark. Their cities, fortresses, temples, and mines could be found across the world, and their sturdy construction meant that they weathered the millennia better than any others. As they were intended to permanently support large populations, most dwarven dungeons were extremely defensible and contained the infrastructure needed to provide housing, food, and water, making them extremely desirable for later occupants. Great dwarven cities like Gauntlgrym and Mithral Hall became massive dungeons, while the military tunnels beneath Fell Pass made for smaller but no less intricate delves.

The duergar built cities in the Underdark every bit as grand as their cousins, and layered them with fortifications and redoubts. Their imperious hostile stance with most of their neighbors ensured constant conflict, demanding that their settlements and colonies be extremely well defended.

Elves
Surface elves rarely built extensively underground, but the ruins of their ancient fortresses, tombs, and cities resulted in many dungeons regardless. The Ardeep Forest was dotted with small ruins, while the High Forest region contained the lost fortress of Nar Kerymhoarth, the ruins of Glaurachyndaar, and Nithrithvinae's Tomb.

Drow-built dungeons were notable in that they tunnelled from the Underdark up to the surface, rather than the other way around. These utilitarian outposts served as fortified trade posts, or military bases for raids on enemies. As both these tasks involved the transfer of prisoners and slaves, they were well-equipped with holding pens.

Dragons
Dragons lived in spacious lairs that housed themselves, their eggs, and their hoard in safety. Though they sometimes excavated spaces by their own efforts, they were far more likely to move into pre-existing natural caverns or forcefully take over dungeons built by others. Every dragon's lair contained at least one large central chamber that they could fly and fight in, and (unless the dragon could teleport) a hidden access tunnel that the dragon could fly or swim through to reach the outside world. Any draconic servants lived in connected caverns or nearby surface villages.

Beholders

Beholders built elaborate, expansive lairs to fulfil their sense of paranoia and grandeur, and store their many trophies and treasures. They enslaved creatures using their charm eye beams, and carved out large areas themselves using their disintegrate beams. These lairs contained vertical shafts and other architecture designed for the beholder to float along, making them much harder to navigate for non-flying intruders. They also tended to contain small armies of minions such as humans, kobolds, goblinoids, ogres, and orcs, as well as collections of exotic pets such as basilisks, otyughs, and smaller, weaker beholderkin like gazers.

Creator Races
The creator races built the oldest surviving dungeons in the Realms. Their ancient strongholds were hidden and went largely unnoticed by the world, containing ancient lore and artifacts guarded by powerful liches and armies of servitors. Hidden outposts like Oreme and the Hall of Mists served as dangerous remnants of those long-gone ancient empires.

Eccentric individuals and conspiratorial groups built hidden strongholds that were strongly defended against the outside world. Wealthy adventurers built fortified homes, reclusive mages created magically-warded studies and laboratories, and organizations such as the Cult of the Dragon and Zhentarim maintained networks of hidden bases to operate from in hostile territory. These dungeons were typically well-designed and well-appointed with both defenses and treasures, and were linked via portals to allow rapid and secret access.

Halflings & Gnomes
Halflings and gnomes were not famous dungeon-builders, but they did tend to build shallow underground homes, mines, and redoubts hidden in the sides of hills. Though hardly as elaborate or massive as others, on the occasions that they were abandoned they made fine enough dungeons.

Humans
Humans and other surface-dwelling civilizations were less likely to build elaborate underground complexes, but dungeons did emerge from the ruins of their fallen castles, cities and tombs. Many of these smaller dungeons were the cellars and catacombs of surface structures, and had the potential to be extremely dangerous due to the ill-kept ruins collapsing in on themselves. In contrast, the great ancient magical empires of humanity—including Netheril, Jhaamdath, Narfell, and Imaskar—built many of the most incredible dungeons known in later ages, such as Dun-Tharos, the Celestial Nadir, and Ythryn.

Intents

Many dungeons were the abandoned and ruined remnants of larger structures, fallen to disrepair and home to new inhabitants. Castles, temples, and tombs were often made of sturdy stone, built into well-designed underground spaces, and intended to withstand warfare, disasters, and the passage of time, and so survived physically after they were abandoned and forgotten. Many dungeons were thus the incidental remnants of the past, their original purpose long lost.

 Cragmaw Castle consisted of a single large structure that had partially collapsed, though intact enough to have distinct internal areas.
 Sarbreen was a great dwarven city that was destroyed by orcs, and its ruins were then built atop by the city of Ravens Bluff. Sarbreen remained partially intact beneath the new city, and still contained treasures and monsters within its secret passages.

Other dungeons were created wholesale as defensive structures, intended to dissuade intruders and kill those who pressed on. These could be the secure vaults of temples and palaces, the warded towers of wizards, sanctified tombs, sturdy fortifications, or the lairs of powerful creatures such as dragons and beholders. Whether or not in continual use by the builders, they were home to well-designed defensive architecture, ingenious mechanisms and traps, and magical defenses such as simple alarms, summoned monsters, and warding against scrying and teleportation.

 The dooms of liches were built to act as the home, study, stronghold and vault of the resident lich. As the final redoubt of powerful and often mad spellcasters, they were equipped with equally fearsome traps, monsters, spells, and servants dedicated to defending the lich's treasures and phylactery.
 The underground fortress of Karak was an immense, abandoned structure, filled with treasures and monsters.

Some rare dungeons were specifically created to lure in and kill intruders. This could be for strategic goals, or the personal malevolent fancies of the builder.

 The lich Acererak created dungeons across many worlds, stocked them with treasures and monsters, and used them to attract adventurers and heroes. Whether for his curiosity, cruelty, or fuelling his phylactery with their souls, the intruders would invariably be destroyed by the defenses within. The Tomb of Nine Gods was a prominent example in Chult of Faerûn.
 The House of Stone was a dwarven fortress that intentionally invited invading armies inside so that they could be destroyed by traps, hostile architecture, and a small team of defenders. Even after its abandonment, it remained a formidable challenge for explorers and looters.
 Xammux and the Library of Tarchamus was an elaborate, spiteful trap devised by the Netherese arcanist Tarchamus that was intended to lure in and kill his rivals, and anyone else who was unlucky enough to explore it.

Forms

The archetypal dungeon was a ruined surface or underground structure that survived by virtue of its sturdy construction. Underground passages could be specifically excavated for the dungeon's function, repurposed from old mines, or be built into existing natural caverns.

 The Temple of the Splendor of Splendors was a large underground temple in Chessenta that was overtaken by many successive groups of people and monsters.
 Warlock's Crypt was the fallen Netherese enclave of Orbedal, reclaimed by the lich Larloch and turned into his lair.
 Wave Echo Cave was a prime example of a dungeon, as it was a surface-linked dwarven mine that took advantage of existing natural cave formations.

Some dungeons were simply natural formations that were convenient to inhabit. These were usually caves carved out by natural forces, then inhabited by successive creatures ranging from common bandits, to gangs of trolls, to dragons. They could have artificial workings, but their form was defined by twisting tunnels and chambers, potentially leading deep underground to the Underdark.

 The Caverns of the Claws attracted trolls from the Stonelands to lair in its depths.
 The Dragon's Eye was a winding network of volcanic tunnels and caverns in Icewind Dale, whose warmth drew lizardfolk and other creatures.
 The Endless Caverns beneath the Star Mounts were home to dragons and other creatures.

Surface dungeons were also found in the Realms. They originated as expansive structures or even whole cities that fell to ruin and formed a maze of overgrown streets, wide foundations, crumbling stone walls, and partially intact buildings. Thanks to their sheer size they could contain smaller dungeons within their greater whole.

 Balanzia in T'u Lung was a great ruined city, destroyed by war and haunted by its former inhabitants.
 Mussum in Chondath was abandoned due to plague and never re-inhabited. The ruins remained untouched due to the sickness persisting over centuries.
 Omu in Chult was destroyed when its people were enslaved by the lich Acererak then buried alive in the Tomb of the Nine Gods.
 Tempat Larang was the southern capital of Anok-Imaskar, and was buried by volcanic ash and lava.

Dungeons could also be contained within extradimensional demiplanes as a means of ensuring security and gaining additional room to work with, or made up of physically disconnected spaces linked by portals. Though rare, their unusual origins typically meant that they were stocked with magical defenses, enchanted treasures, arcane lore, and terrifying guardians. They could also have extreme, unintended changes to their form, as the demiplanes or portals took on new properties due to their magic warping or failing over time.

 The Celestial Nadir was an Imaskari demiplane used for experimentation, storage, and transport.
 Holdfast was an ancient elven hideout made up of portal-connected spaces, protected by magic and constructs.
 The Wizard's Pit beneath the ruins of Crownpost was made up of numerous portal-connected spaces.

Ecology

Unusual creatures often made their homes in dungeons, as they were able to exploit the peculiarities of a dungeon environment to the fullest extent. The bizarre biology of such creatures meant that they were rarely found elsewhere, though dungeons were also inhabited by common surface creatures such as rats, small insects, and bats. Larger surface animals were also known to adapt to deep and spacious dungeons, with packs of wolves and flocks of birds living part-time or full-time underground, while underground pools, rivers, and lakes were home to home to fish and other aquatic animals. After many generations of living in darkness, they tended to develop darkvision or other adaptions that helped them survive.

The builders of dungeons sometimes kept exotic zoological or botanical collections that could escape their confines once the dungeon fell to ruin. Likewise, if a dungeon included portals that connected to distant locales, it could be home to incongruous creatures that wandered through and laired in the new location. Many dungeons were connected to the Underdark, and so were inhabited by subterranean monsters close to the surface.

 Carrion crawlers enjoyed moist, dark, and filthy spaces, and so often lived inside of dungeons.
 Cave fishers were well-adepted to ambushing prey in the Underdark, and this translated to dungeons that gave them enough space to lurk in.
 Darkmantles disguised themselves as rocky stalagmites before attacking.
 Gelatinous cubes filled a cubic space and were well-adapted to slowly travelling along regular stone passages, digesting any edible matter found along the way.
 Lurkers hid and ambushed prey from the ceilings of caves and tunnels.
 Mimics used their shapechanging to hunt, and had the intelligence to take the form of attractive objects such as treasure chests that adventurers and looters would be drawn to.
 Oozes such as gray oozes, ochre jellies, and black puddings often inhabited dungeons. They lurked in choice areas and allowed prey to blunder into them, removing the need for active hunting.
 Otyughs made their homes in middens and sewers, and would happily devour unfortunate explorers.
 Piercers were almost indistinguishable from stalactites and could only hunt from an overhead ceiling, limiting them to underground caverns and structures.
 Ropers resembled stalagmites, and ambushed prey from this disguise.
 Rust monsters were pacifistic and devoured metals, finding ideal homes in treasure-stocked dungeons visited by metal-carrying intruders. Though unlikely to kill adventurers on their own, they were feared and despised for their habit of destroying the weapons and armor used to fend off other threats.
 Trappers could use limited shapeshifting to disguise themselves as entire floors, walls, and ceilings, and then surround and crush prey.

Molds, slimes, and fungi were omnipresent in dark spaces with a modicum of damp, and were especially prevalent in natural caverns. The wide variety of mushrooms, yeasts, and mildews were the basic food supply for grazing herbivores and omnivores, and could also act as phosphorescent light sources or be dangerously toxic to eat. Some had extraordinary abilities and posed potentially lethal threats to adventurers or anything else that lived underground:

 Brown mold fed on heat, and could suck the warmth out of living creatures.
 Green slime was feared and hated, as it dripped down on prey and quickly reproduced, devouring all organic matter.
 Shrieker mushrooms grew underground and used their piercing scream to attract creatures that would then be killed by other predators. They were sometimes cultivated by residents as living alarm systems.
 Violet fungi often cohabited with shriekers and used their flailing tentacles to poison nearby prey.
 Yellow mold expelled a cloud of poisonous spores that could fill entire tunnels.

Tools

In addition to weapons, healing supplies, and a good supply of food and water, dungeon-delvers were well advised to bring specialized equipment along to explore and survive:

 10-foot poles and collapsible poles, to probe the way ahead and test for traps.
 Ball bearings, to test for slopes and other irregularities, or slip up foes.
 Chalk, to mark paths and leave messages.
 Climber's kits, to safely ascend or descend perilous heights.
 Crowbars, to force open doors, boxes and other obstacles.
 Drills, for boring through wood or even metal and stone given enough time.
 Holy water, for destroying fiends and undead, or reconsecrating holy sites.
 Magnets, for manipulating small metal objects.
 Silver or steel mirrors, for looking around corners, avoiding visual petrification, or signalling with light.
 Oil, to fuel lights, burn obstacles, and finish off trolls.
 Rope, chain, wire, and twine, for climbing, securing items and captives, or making traps.
 Rubbing kits, to make copies of graven text and images for later investigation.
 Scroll cases, for protecting maps, records, and magical scrolls from water damage.
 Spikes and pitons, to jam doors shut or use as climbing aids.
 Thieves' tools, to finesse locks, traps, and other mechanical devices.
 Tongs, for safely grabbing and manipulating things at a distance.
 Tools such as shovels picks, hammers, and saws, for breaking through obstables or building new constructions.
 Torches, candles, or lanterns (bullseye, hooded, or regular) to light the way and start fires.

Magical items had an unending number of uses in dungeons, but some proved more reliably useful than others.
 Bag of tricks, to produce an animal servant on the spot.
 Bag of holding or portable hole, to easily carry in large amounts of supplies and carry out large amounts of treasure.
 Dust of dryness, to reduce flooding and store water for later.
 Immovable rod, to provide the perfect anchor point.
 Nolzur's marvelous pigments, for producing quick solutions to problems.
 Ring of X-ray vision, to see through walls and doors safely.
 Sovereign glue, to definitively bind or seal a door or mechanism.

Traps & Hazards

Traps were used to passively deal with intruders to dungeons, and could be of magical or mechanical design. Most dungeons contained chokepoints like doors and narrow passages, and so provided many opportunities for cunning trapcraft. The simplest traps like bell-alarms and mounted crossbows found use by practical-minded kobolds and bandits, while elaborate devices like rotating drum chambers and summoned outsiders were the domain of those with too much time and money. They were were best used to support active defenders who were intimately familiar with the battleground and could lure attackers into prepared positions and force them to deal with both at the same time. Magical dangers included the unpredictable phenomena caused by the decay or malfunction of magical traps and defenses, and living spells could be found roaming dungeons as magic ran rampant or experimentations went awry.

Artificial constructs were mostly found in the ruins of temples, palaces, and wizard towers, as they were an expensive but durable defense that avoided many of the weaknesses of living defenders, while being more proactive and adaptive than traps. Creations like golems and helmed horrors were often limited to attacking intruders that approached their designated area, and remained there until finally destroyed.

The undead were a common threat found in dungeons, whether animated by stray magical energies, part of an intended defensive measure, or raised by opportunistic necromancers. Zombies, skeletons, shadows, and ghosts could be found haunting the tombs where they were interred or wandering the sites that they died, while ghouls and ghasts lurked in hidden lairs while searching for carrion. More powerful and intelligent undead such as wights, vampires, mummies, and liches tended to take over or build entire dungeons to serve their purposes, and used legions of lesser undead as their servants.

Environmental Dangers

Enclosed underground dungeons were prone to natural and incidental dangers, many of which were shared in common with the Underdark, especially if the complex linked to or was inside the Underdark.

 Cave-ins were a threat anywhere underground, capable of crushing or trapping creatures. They could be triggered by natural geological shifts, or more dramatically as part of magical battles that caused great shocks and impacts. Natural caverns and poorly-shored mines were the most likely to collapse in on themselves, while well-designed underground habitation such as dwarven cities were usually quite resistant to structural failure.
 Falls were a worry in proximity to the edges of monolithic structures and deep chasms. These dangers were exacerbated if combat broke out in such a location.
 Fires posed a threat not only for the burns they could inflict, but for the consumption of oxygen in confined spaces that could suffocate living creatures. Fortunately, as many dungeons were long-abandoned or carved directly out of the earth, burnable materials such as wood were not necessarily present.
 Flooding was a terrible threat in confined spaces, particularly underground. Air-breathers (which included most adventurers) could easily be drowned as chambers and tunnels filled with water.
 Temperatures could vary wildly throughout underground dungeons, even without accounting for magical effects. Underdark lakes could be numbingly cold, while geothermal heat could raise temperatures to lethal highs.
 Toxic gases could disorient, incapacitate, or kill living creatures, especially in confined spaces. Gas could occur naturally from the environment such as volcanism, or be from artifice such as traps. Some noxious gases could even explode, threatening to start fires and cause structural collapse.
 Volcanism caused a variety of dangers in addition to the obvious threat of exposed magma or lava flows. Volcanism led to earthquakes and cave-ins, toxic gases, bursts of steam, and bodies of superheated water.

Appendix
Behind the Scenes

Dungeons are used within the context of Dungeons & Dragons as a means of presenting adventure gameplay such as puzzles, traps, combat encounters, and social interactions in a series of discrete scenes much like a flowchart. This allows the Dungeon Master to plan, anticipate, and respond to the players more effectively, as there is a semi-linear order to these scenes. The conceit of a classic dungeon as a series of underground rooms connected by hallways with obstacles such as locked doors, traps, and monsters along the way is intended to facilitate this gameplay style, and it can be extended to structures that are very much not dungeons. This guided, semi-constrained gameplay separates dungeons from the random encounters and freeform exploration that could take place elsewhere in the "open world."

## Durapatya

Durapatya was a town in the Kuong Kingdom in Malatra.

History
When Priest-King Vishnan IV discovered a spring that produced elixir of youth, he established a temple and town nearby to give thanks to the Lords of Creation.

Description
Durapatya was a small town constructed around the temple.

Activities
By the mid–14 century DR, the magic of the spring had faded, yet pilgrims and others seeking cures still flocked there.

## Dungeons & Dragons Online: Menace of the Underdark

Dungeons and Dragons Online: Menace of the Underdark is an expansion pack for Dungeons & Dragons Online released in June 2012. The expansion is primarily set in the Forgotten Realms, and focuses on a drow plot to conquer the town of Eveningstar and find the Thread of the Weave.

Plot
Web of Chaos
Beyond the Rift introduces Faerûn proper, as the various forces of Eberron assign the player with the goal of entering the Rift Between Worlds to the Demonweb Pits and finding out what lies on the other side, in the hopes of closing the portal before it devours Eberron whole. It also introduces Ana Brabener, an Evenor commoner who is rescued from drow captivity when the players emerge in Faerûn's Underdark.

The Darkening

Impossible Demands sees Helena Falagon call for aid in rescuing the Midler family from the mercies of Vicala Szind, a cruel priestess. The situation is complicated by Vicala's ritualistic life-draining, which lets her sacrifice the Midlers and her own followers in order to restore her own health. This requires the players avoid combat with her and instead slay her followers and free the prisoners first, then confront her.

The Unquiet Graves has Barana Talmere send out the player to Deep Glen in the King's Forest and stop a team of House Szind necromancers from raising not just slain villagers, but the entire contents of the ancient graveyard.

The Lost Thread is started by Cormul Haythorne, who begs the players to go rescue Ana Brabener for a second time. Cormul, Ana, and Torin Betrich had explored the Temple of Mystra in the hopes of finding the "Thread of the Weave" the drow were demanding, only for Ana to be caught by them a second time. The players thus have to go rescue her from the depths of the ruins, fighting off many drow and breaking their barriers thanks to her unexpected powerful magical talent.

The Battle for Eveningstar is introduced by Swordcaptain Garwyn Thane. The drow return to Eveningstar to finish it off in one great attack, and the player has to turn it into defeat for them. Fighting through the combined forces of Szind, Dun'Robar, and Avithoul, the players take the fight to their base camp and encounter the field commander Draya Dun'Robar riding atop the bebilith Red Weaver. With their forces scattered and their commanders dead, the drow forces are unable to continue fighting and give up on attacking Eveningstar, at least for the time being.

City of Portals

The next story arc sends the player into the Underdark below the King's Forest in search of the drow city of Sschindylryn, accessed by a portal opened near Eveningstar. After a lengthy journey, the player can finally find the city itself and strike at the three main houses that support the drow invasion, wounding them and setting them against each other.

The House of Broken Chains is started by Mirini Elemys, escaped slave. She sends the player into House Avithoul's estate disguised as a slave in order to start a break-out for the other slaves, destroy the house's treasures, and kill Overseer Solul. This breaks the power base of House Avithoul, and causes them to regard their former allies with suspicion that they were behind the sabotage.

The House of Rusted Blades is performed on behalf of War Wizard Traegar Vadamore, who seeks to cripple House Dun'Robar, the most militaristic of the three houses. The player is illusioned to resemble a drow servant, giving them the opportunity to sneak about the estate and sabotage Dun'Robar's prized weapons. On the way out, the player encounter and defeat Matron Jyr Dun'Robar and her High Battlemaster Teblyn Dun'Robar. With their weapons ruined and champions defeated, Dun'Robar loses both power and prestige and looks with suspicion on its allies.

The House of Death Undone is prompted by priestess Oryne Belamadin, who wishes to remove House Szind's force of undead and necromancers. Using captured flashstones, the player is able to destroy Szind's necromantic cauldrons and stop their undead production, in addition to wrecking the household the normal way. The player also has an option to get into intra-house politics by overhearing Priestess Silan Szind plotting with the wizard Sarnish about overthrowing her mother Matron Zhivsha Szind, who is surprisingly grateful to be told about it by the player. Whatever happens, Szind's capabilities are ruined and their ability to project power is crippled.

The Portal Opens occurs once all three houses are weakened and infighting, and the diviner Jenneth Gosmere sends the player on a mission with Elminster and Ana Brabener into the Temple of Lolth atop Sschindylryn to stop High Priestess Hekta Szind. The team fights through the Sschindylryn Ziggurat, overcomes its elite defenders, and has Elminster break through the temple defenses while the others move on without him. Meeting Hekta, she identifies Ana as the Thread of the Weave and magically compels her to step into a portal to the Demonweb Pits. After a fierce fight, Hekta is overcome and begs Lolth for mercy, who responds with disgust and sends a demon to execute her.

The Queen of the Demonweb

With the immediate material aspect of the drow threat resolved, the player is next sent into another portal near Eveningstar that leads directly into the Demonweb Pits to oppose Lolth and her servants in their home territory. The ultimate goal is the rescue of Ana Brabener (for a third time) before Lolth can take Mystra's power from her, preventing her from becoming the new goddess of magic. Do do so, the first step is the capture of several orbs of Lolth, which weaken Ana and prevent her from escaping.

Trial by Fury is started by War Wizard Walak, who sends the players into the Domain of Lord Grulemith, where Grulemith protects an orb of Lolth. Fighting through a labyrinth of acid-drenched islands filled with mental and physical challenges, Grulemith is eventually challenged directly and slain, leaving the orb vulnerable.

The Deal and the Demon involves the arcanaloth Ymris, who has already been hired to kill the demon Kishnaurac, and invites the players along to steal the orb of Lolth while his mercenaries assist. After fighting through the icy Realm of Ruin, Kishnaurac offers the yugoloth mercenaries a higher pay, causing them to instantly flip and attack the players.

Reclaiming the Rift sends the players back to the Rift Between Worlds, where the draegloth Khaszlokhar guards the Rift as well as an orb. The sage Valana has devised a magical enchantment that will stop the Rift from growing, though it requires a significant blood sacrifice on the players' part. Despite this handicap, the players are able to slay Khaszlokhar, stabilize the Rift, and take the orb.

Caught in the Web
Caught in the Web has Elminster send the players into Lolth's domain to rescue Ana and get her out before Lolth steals Mystra's divinity. After breaking Ana out of her bonds, she is escorted to more orbs of Lolth in order to steal back Mystra's power and diminish Lolth's, even while Lolth personally intervenes to crush the players. After finding a portal back to the Prime Material plane, the players and Ana escape into the Underdark and face Lolth one last time before Ana blasts her back into the Abyss.

Other Quests
Several quests are disconnected from the main plot, and are stand-alone stories that run alongside the ongoing war.

Murder by Night leads the player to the Eveningstar War Hospital, where Delethir Mistveil requests aid in an investigation of the murdered head priest Hurim Jaskyr. The player quickly finds that lycanthropy is spreading from infected soldiers brought from the King's Forest, leading to a running battle as patients try to escape. The leader of the pack, Ogiyon Tomain, eventually appears to avenge his kin, only to fall in turn.

The Riddle starts with Ingval Muth, who sends the player to investigate a burnt-out ruin on the edge of town that Wizard of War Barthand disappeared into. The threat within turns out to be the night hag Frelga, working with the drow attackers to plague the local's dreams and try to find the Thread of the Weave. Barthand turned out to have been transformed into a large toadstool and kept as a trophy by Frelga's coven of green hags. After rescuing Barthand and destroying Frelga's detached magical eye, the night hag was caught and destroyed, bringing an end to her prophetic ramblings.

Don't Drink the Water is prompted by the mason Unmar Halberg, who's determined that Eveningstar's well has somehow been poisoned. He sends the player down to the cavern it draws from, only to find a team of yuan-ti agents behind it. After passing some maddened subterranean dryads, the player discovers the true source of the toxins: the green dragon Tharmalos, hired by the drow to weaken Eveningstar from within.

In the Belly of the Beast has Sorina Parnock send the players into the Underdark to find the Cay'Vorym Fighting Pit, where captured Purple Dragons are being thrown into death matches for entertainment. On arrival the player is welcomed as a potential customer, and has the opportunity to interfere and commit intrigue among the other contestants and customers. After finding the imprisoned Purple Dragons, Feryl Cay'Vorym drops everyone into the pit, setting them against various gladiators and prisoners, culminating in purple worms being attracted to the battle. Finally, the massive worm Sineater tears into the arena, eats most of the audience, destroys the structure, and swallows both Feryl and the players. The players are forced to navigate the cavernous guts of the worm, dodging pools of digestive acid, roaming parasites, and corrosive phlegm, until they find Feryl and his sword Earthcarver, which they use to cut their way out of the worm and finally kill it.

## Dungeons & Dragons Online: Shadowfell Conspiracy

Dungeons and Dragons Online: Shadowfell Conspiracy is an expansion pack for Dungeons & Dragons Online released in August 2013. The expansion is primarily set in the Forgotten Realms, and focuses on a Netherese plot to destroy Cormyr using the Nether Scrolls.

Plot
Shadowfell Conspiracy is introduced by the Disciples of Shadow adventure pack, which involves the capture of the notorious Sharran priest Dedryk Black and his transportation to Wheloon Prison, before turning to the wider plot. The plot takes place roughly at the same time as Menace of the Underdark, and the content is broken into two main quest chains: Shadow over Wheloon, and The Secret of the Storm Horns. The two halves of Shadowfell Conspiracy are bridged by the adventure packs The Druid's Deep and The High Road of Shadows, which deal with the consequences of Oriphaun Huntsilver's actions after leaving Wheloon. The Shadow Under Thunderholme pack features the Netherese in a parallel but separate plot focused on the Cult of the Dragon.

Iconic characters are introduced in the expansion, which begin with a starting class, a large amount of experience with which to reach level 15, and a full set of special items to be equipped with. Faerûnian options include a renegade shadar-kai rogue, a disgraced human Purple Dragon knight fighter, and an overeager Amaunatori sun elf priest, who all start in the garret of the Ball and Chain Tavern, where Elminster meets with them to discuss their future before they charge into the world.

Disciples of Shadow
Shadow of a Doubt is the final quest of Disciples of Shadow and requires Shadowfell Conspiracy to access. It has the players perform an interrogation of Dedryk at the Magistrate's Office on the docks on behalf of Celia Pendrick and Cornelius Latrimor. The interrogation is interrupted by the Shadowfell Nexus briefly dropping them into the Shadowfell, allowing a horde of shadows to attack. The players go look for Watch Captain Hillard, fighting past more shadows and rioting prisoners that had escaped from the prison, rescuing several Purple Dragons and protecting a docked ship along the way. After securing the area, the players return to the Magistrate's Office, only to find Cornelius Latrimor dead and Dedryk Black vanished, thanks to the infiltration of the assassin Teralyn.

Shadow Over Wheloon

The Wheloon Prison is filled with hostile prisoners, monsters, and Shadovar agents, with the danger increasing the closer one gets to Oldstone Hall due to the Shadovar invasion of Wheloon. Players may be randomly dropped in and out of the Shadowfell version of the area, which functions as a similar but separate map. Scattered throughout the zone are notes and journal entries left by Oriphaun Huntsilver (on Heroic difficulty) and Archwizard Tyvrus (on Epic difficulty), which add details and context to the plot and wider world beyond the quests. The quest chain is provided by Davny Selkin (on Heroic) and Edric Crastmere (on Epic).

Friends in Low Places involves Faline Abbot sending the players into the city to aid the Wheloon natives, a group of non-Sharran inmates trapped in the prison since its creation based at Harvest Hall. In order to gain the trust of the group, the players assist the natives living in Wheloon's drainage culverts, who are being attacked by a violent gang of other inmates. Ultimately the native encampments are protected, while the gang led by Porto, Athea, and Aramiya Troise is defeated.

In A Lesson in Deception, Gaspry Bennik sends the players to assist a team of infiltrators investigating unusual gang activity in the prison. The spies led by Fordwin are captured by the Sharran priest Stanwick Galle, who has been unifying the disparate prisoners into a single large gang for Thultanthar. The players fight through the prisoners and their allies to defeat the foul priest, potentially causing the deaths of the captured Cormyrean spies or other innocents as they proceed.

The Thrill of the Hunt has Hargrove Jalt send the players to the merchant's townhouse that is home to the sadistic shadar-kai Karleth, who has captured the Magistrates Aloysius Cornwall and Fledren Hull. After overcoming the shadar-kai and their pets, the players defeat Karleth, hopefully before he kills his prisoners.

Army of Shadow is started by Iden Krell, who sends the players to infiltrate and attack the Netherese recruitment center, where the shade General Marthir arms and indoctrinates the prisoners into his army. After dropping the charade, the players take out his underlings Rantha and Darobard to unlock the gate to his tower, then face Marthir and his nightcrawler, stopping the Netherese recruitment inside the prison.

Through a Mirror Darkly ends the Shadovar invasion when Jacinta Last sends the players into Oldstone Hall to meet Oriphaun Huntsilver, kill Archwizard Tyvrus, and recover his Nether Scroll. The building is protected by physical and shadowy barriers, requiring the players to jump between material and shadow realms to bypass the defenses and get to Tyvrus. Though Tyvrus himself does the same, he is defeated and Oriphaun absconds with the Nether Scroll.

The Secret of the Storm Horns

The Storm Horns region appears peaceful, but the forests and mountains hide a sizable Netherese army as they work towards a plot to destroy Cormyr. The local wildlife are also overly hostile due to the poaching and logging performed by the Netherese forces. After escaping Wheloon and disposing of the Nether Scroll, Oriphaun has returned to the Storm Horns to uncover the Netherese plot in the area. Notes are scattered throughout the area by Oriphaun Huntsilver (on Heroic difficulty) and Archwizard Tyvrus (on Epic difficulty), which elaborate on the plot further. The quest chain is provided by Kara Patwin (on Heroic) and Lorant Tass (on Epic).

The Tracker's Trap sees Nevyle Dunwaite organize a meeting between the players and Oriphaun at Yarrow Glade, only to be attacked by Netherese forces. Oriphaun is captured while the players fight their way out of the ambush, overcoming the arcanist Shai on the way out. They may also aid the hermit Haurugg, who has been displaced by the invaders.

In Lines of Supply, the players head to Tumblerock Pass, sent by Rina the Keen-Blade to meet Kyra Armond there. The mission is to destroy the convoys the Netherese are using to move supplies into the mountains, potentially with the assistance of local harpies. After destroying the convoys and their escorts, the players ultimately face the enemy commander Grablandurr.

Breaking the Ranks has Orin Arroway ask the players to investigate the Netherese central camp high in the mountains. Facing waves of gnolls, orcs, hill giants, and shadar-kai, the players eventually face and defeat their leader Draeton. The dracolich Arricaxerxes observes the fighting, throwing some blasts of ice down before leaving.

In A Break In the Ice, Renton Finne sends the players to meet the frost giant Rungnir in his glacier, a former ally of the Netherese who has become rebellious. The players find that Rungnir has broken entirely with the Netherese and hidden in the depths of the glacier, and join him on an attack on his nemesis, the wizard Tharno. He proves an unreliable ally, and arrogantly turns on the players afterwards to assert control over his domain.

What Goes Up concludes the adventure, and has Sherrod Starym task the players with investigating deeper into the glacier to find Oriphaun. After finding him and freeing him, it is revealed that the grand project the Netherese are working on is a fully intact and functional mythallar buried in the glacier. Although the players partially interrupt the ritual being worked on it, the Netherese succeed in reactivating it and turning the whole glacier into a new flying enclave with which to assault Cormyr with. After breaking the enclave's flight pillars and killing Amskar, Oriphaun and the players jump off as the enclave flies out of control and smashes into a mountainside, ending the Netherese invasion plans.

## Dultann

Dultann was a small village on the island of Gwynneth.

Description
Dultann consisted of a pond owned collectively by the residents, a woodcarver's shop, and a tavern that were clustered among a few sheep farms.

Geography
This village was located on the eastern edge of the Myrloch Vale.

History
In 1359 DR,Dragon #362 provides an exact date for the events of Halls of the High King, which did not give an exact date within itself. the inhabitants of this village were wracked with fear over tales of the Risen Cult of Bane and their leader the "Dark Druid". Around that time the cult's priests Anphrantul and Oishannan were living in the village, along with other informants of the cult.

Notable Locations
 Dultann's Beard, a tavern run by Paedrac Rollstone in the 14 century DR.

## Dust of illusion

Dust of illusion was a type of enchanted dust that could disguise a creature.

Description
Unless stared at intently, this dust resembled powdered graphite or chalkdust. But when it was under scrutiny, dust of illusion would change color and form. It was contained in pouches, which usually contained around elven to twenty pinches.

Powers
Applying a pinch of dust of illusion to any creature allowed it to appear as any other creature of a similar shape, allowing for a size variance of around 50%. So for example, a human could appear as an ogre and a pegasus could appear as a mule.

While applying dust of illusion to a creature, the wielder had to envision the desired illusion. Unless dispelled, this illusionary effect would last for around seven to twelve hours.

Creation
Crystalmists could be used to make a high-quality dust of illusion.

Notable Owners
 Halbasho.
 Haroistem.
 Torm of the Knights of Myth Drannor was a notorious hoarder of magic devices and trinkets. He owned some dust of illusion and sometimes kept it on his person.

## Dust of dispersion

Dust of dispersion was a type of magical powder designed for combat situations.

Powers
A handful of this dust thrown into the air would create a translucent cloud, roughly  across in every direction. Creatures were capable of seeing into and through the cloud, though those looking through it would have their vision slightly blurred. While this was up in the air, any ranged attacks made against a creature standing inside the cloud of dust would have a lower probability of hitting. This negative probability effect lessened over time.

On average the cloud caused by a dust of dispersion would remain in the air for three minutes, though it could be dispersed quicker by moderate to strong winds. Fire-based spells could also burn away parts of it. And the dust's effects would not work underwater. Once used, dust of dispersion became inert and thus could not be reused.

Creation
Crafting a dust of dispersion required one to know the spells blur and glitterdust. And it cost around 250 gold pieces.

## Dungeons & Dragons Online/The Mines of Tethyamar

The Mines of Tethyamar is an adventure pack for Dungeons & Dragons Online where the player characters are lured to the ruins of Tethyamar by dwarven promises of riches ripe for the taking, but end up delving too deep for their own good. The adventure pack was added to the game on July 12, 2017 as part of Update 36.

Plot

In The Mines of Tethyamar the story is introduced by Asta the Deep-Minded, a concerned elder dwarf who has noticed the odd events surrounding the expedition to Tethyamar. She assigns the players the responsibility of helping out the other dwarves as they struggle against the drow and their own destructive urges, and uncovering the hidden plans behind the expedition.

Third Time's a Charm is started by Eluf the Steadfast, who reports that many dwarves have been taken captive by hostile drow operating in the nearby tunnels. Even worse, the rescue mission sent after them has also been captured. The scout Rielle Proudforge escaped to tell of the disaster, and so the players are sent to rescue them in turn. Fighting past the drow slavers Uhlsirayn, Phyrsinil, and Phy'xzar, the dwarves are freed from their bonds… though they refuse to leave, insisting they must stay to finish mining the jewels of ardor found in the tunnels there.

Graveyard Shift is begged upon the player by Hagen Silverthumb, who tells that he only just escaped Egil Hakon, who had gone mad and killed his fellow miners while working in Tethyamar Mineshaft 214S. Traveling down into the pit, the player encounters malfunctioning mining golems, furious elementals, and the undead remains of many, many fallen dwarves. Egil Hakon is found lurking among the dead, and possessed of strange magical powers he should not have. With his death, one of many crimes in Tethyamar is avenged.

Records of the Past, by the surveyor Freygar Plumbline, has the players go down into Ironvale Deep where Tethyamar's archived records were stored. He wishes to find the Tethyamar Master Manifest, which describes all the treasures found in Tethyamar's mines, and use it to more safely find and mine the jewels of ardor. The ruined settlement is filled with restless undead, hungry monsters, and an expedition of drow also searching for the Manifest. After facing their leader Kir'tan Melarn, the Manifest is recovered and brought back to Freygar for examination. After a look-through he decides that the Manifest is falsified and useless, as it only describes the jewels of ardor as low-value crystals not worth pursuing.

Strike Back has Gunhild the Watchful send the player on the offensive against the drow stronghold nearby. Though well defended, they are able to fight their way through and take out the High Priestess Talice Argith along with her followers Ildan Argith, Lieutenant Rauva, Captain Zyn, and slave master Velkas. A number of slaves can be freed, and an interesting guest, Alystin Dyrr can be chatted with. Despite destroying the drow force and saving many slaves, Gunhild is upset that the player did not take the time to gather more jewels of ardor.

Desire in the Dark starts with the frantic Inger Ingersdottir, who reports that Hal Stoutheart has disappeared on a walk in the Eastern Reaches. With the entire expedition resting on his capable shoulders, it's vital to get him back. The players find many dwarves and drow fighting to the death over the jewels of ardor, spectated by a cruel and amused Hal. He leads them further into the depths, all the way to Faraway. The players are able to untangle the illusions at play and rescue fellow victims Silent the drow and Zoraxzil the mind flayer, infuriating Graz'zt at the disrespect of deviating from his script. Past Faraway, the players grind through tunnels filled with fiend worms that Graz'zt had raised, before finally meeting the demon lord properly in his luxurious lair. After demonstrating all the guises and schemes he had used in Tethyamar, Graz'zt engages in a perfunctory battle for his own entertainment before departing with a flourish, leaving some gifts for his dance partners while promising to return and cause an even bigger disaster. Just beyond Graz'zt's lair the players find the mortal remains of the real Hal Stoutheart in Blue Stone Cavern, who died in the caves years ago while exploring. With Graz'zt gone the jewels of ardor lose their intoxicating qualities, returned to simple crystals, and the dwarves are freed from their maniacal compulsions.
